
=== story.oldMillRoad ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005000 · State of North Carolina v. Belinda Park
  Charges: Felony Death by Vehicle; Reckless Driving
  Defendant: Belinda Park, 29, Line cook; priors: 0; Released on a $225,000 secured bond with electronic monitoring.
  TEMPLATE story.oldMillRoad
  Summary: At about 11:52 p.m. on 9 December, Belinda Park's Honda Civic crossed the center line on a curve on Old Mill Road outside Bayard and struck a Toyota Corolla head-on. The Corolla's driver, Alex Fitzgerald, 34, a nurse driving home from a twelve-hour shift, died at the scene. Belinda Park, a line cook at Riverside Grill, had stayed after closing that night. A hospital blood test about ninety minutes after the crash and a second, State Crime Laboratory test about three hours after it disagree on whether Park was over the legal limit when the cars collided. Park says a deer came out of the tree line and she swerved.
  Verdicts: Felony Death by Vehicle: guilty; Reckless Driving: notGuilty
  [Count 1: Felony Death by Vehicle] Class D Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | Minimum 51, maximum 74 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 51 months (4 yr 3 mo). | Maximum term: 74 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Projected release: no earlier than 2 February 2031, and no later than 2 January 2032. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$15,000.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$1,600.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | State Crime Laboratory fee: US$600.00 | Total: US$17,473.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The evidence was clear, and the court followed it."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Park about next steps."
  Reaction: A relative of Alex Fitzgerald, outside the courtroom: "It's something. We can start to move on."
  News: Guilty: Park case ends in conviction — A Hollis County judge has convicted Belinda Park on 1 of 2 counts of felony death by vehicle, and imposed 51 to 74 months in prison. At about 11:52 p.m. on 9 December, Belinda Park's Honda Civic crossed the center line on a curve on Old Mill Road outside Bayard and struck a Toyota Corolla head-on. Two groups gathered on opposite sides of the courthouse steps. Cassandra Yamamoto: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Park about next steps." A relative of Alex Fitzgerald, outside the courtroom: "It's something. We can start to move on." District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The evidence was clear, and the court followed it." Park was taken into custody in the courtroom. The judgment includes US$17,473.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

=== story.churchBooks ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005010 · State of North Carolina v. Antonia Beaumont
  Charges: Embezzlement ($100,000 or more)
  Defendant: Antonia Beaumont, 46, School bus mechanic; priors: 14; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $250,000 secured bond for 74 days.
  TEMPLATE story.churchBooks
  Summary: For six years Antonia Beaumont, a school bus mechanic for Hollis County Schools, served as the volunteer treasurer of Cedar Hill United Methodist Church in Wexley. When a new trustee asked for the building fund statements, $112,400 was gone, moved over three years in 41 checks and transfers to an account in the name of "DL Property Services." Beaumont says Pastor Harold Peebles approved every payment as short-term loans to the contractor rebuilding the fellowship hall, and that the paperwork was handshakes. The pastor, now retired and in poor health, says no such arrangement existed.
  Verdicts: Embezzlement ($100,000 or more): guilty
  [Count 1: Embezzlement ($100,000 or more)] Class C Felony, prior record level VI | Presumptive range | Minimum 117, maximum 153 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 117 months (9 yr 9 mo). | Maximum term: 153 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Credit for 74 days already served in pretrial confinement (G.S. 15-196.1). | Projected release: no earlier than 20 May 2036, and no later than 20 May 2038. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$112,400.00 | Total: US$112,613.00
  Reaction: ADA Laurel Lancaster: "This is a fair result under the sentencing laws, and it's about public trust."
  Reaction: Beth Broadway: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Beaumont about next steps."
  Reaction: A relative of Beaumont's: "We'll be waiting when it's over."
  News: Verdict in ‘$112,000 and a Handshake’ case: guilty — Superior Court Judge Hale found Antonia Beaumont, 46, guilty, and imposed 117 to 153 months in prison, ending a case that began with an arrest in Wexley. According to court records, for six years Antonia Beaumont, a school bus mechanic for Hollis County Schools, served as the volunteer treasurer of Cedar Hill United Methodist Church in Wexley. Reaction outside the courthouse was split. A relative of Beaumont's: "We'll be waiting when it's over." ADA Laurel Lancaster: "This is a fair result under the sentencing laws, and it's about public trust." Beth Broadway: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Beaumont about next steps." Deputies led Beaumont out a side door. Counting 74 days already served, the earliest possible release is 20 May 2036. The judgment includes US$112,613.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

  Charges: First-Degree Burglary; Felony Larceny; Injury to Real Property
  Defendant: Gary Epps, 24, Retail manager; priors: 2; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $300,000 secured bond for 48 days.
  TEMPLATE story.duplexBurglary
  Summary: At about 3:05 a.m. on 13 January, Joseph McGhee woke to a noise in a duplex on Front Street in Bayard and saw a stranger in the hallway, lit only by a nightlight. The intruder ran out the back door, which had been pried open, taking a laptop, a wallet, and a bottle of prescription pain pills. Four days later Gary Epps sold the laptop to a pawn shop a few miles away. Epps says the laptop came from a man at a gas station who sold it for $80, and that he was home asleep that night.
  Verdicts: First-Degree Burglary: notGuilty; Felony Larceny: notGuilty; Injury to Real Property: guilty
  Condition: no contact with the victim
  Stay-away order: 2 years

  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$900.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$2,200.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | Supervision fees ($40/month): US$480.00 | Total: US$3,853.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The evidence was clear, and the court followed it."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "Epps gets to keep the job at Marlowe Boulevard Home Goods. That matters more than people think."
  Reaction: Joseph McGhee's father, outside the courtroom: "Probation. I don't know what to say."
  Reaction: Epps's grandmother: "We're just grateful to have Epps home tonight."
  News: Split verdict for Epps: guilty on 1 of 3 counts — Gary Epps stood as Judge Hale announced the verdict: guilty on 1 of 3 counts. The judge then imposed a suspended sentence and 12 months of probation. At about 3:05 a.m. on 13 January, Joseph McGhee woke to a noise in a duplex on Front Street in Bayard and saw a stranger in the hallway, lit only by a nightlight. District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The evidence was clear, and the court followed it." Cassandra Yamamoto: "Epps gets to keep the job at Marlowe Boulevard Home Goods. That matters more than people think." Epps's grandmother: "We're just grateful to have Epps home tonight." Epps left the courthouse with family. The defendant was ordered into Drug Treatment Court. === story.kitchenStrangulation ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005030 · State of North Carolina v. Chester Brower
  Charges: Assault Inflicting Physical Injury by Strangulation
  Defendant: Chester Brower, 30, College student; priors: 2; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $11,000 secured bond for 49 days.
  TEMPLATE story.kitchenStrangulation
  Summary: At about 1:09 a.m. on 10 March, Josefina Killian called 911 from a bathroom in the house on Fifth Street that she shared with Chester Brower, saying Brower had choked her in the kitchen. Deputies found red marks on Killian's neck and a broken skillet on the floor; Brower had fresh scratches on the face and forearm. Brower says Killian came at Brower with the skillet after a night of drinking and he grabbed Killian to stop it. By trial, Josefina Killian says she had been drinking heavily and can't remember who started it.
  Verdicts: Assault Inflicting Physical Injury by Strangulation: notGuilty
  Reaction: ADA Deja Cisneros: "We presented the evidence we had. We accept the verdict."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "Brower can finally go home."
  Reaction: A relative of Josefina Killian, outside the courtroom: "We just want to be left alone now."
  Reaction: A former employer of Brower's: "I'm taking my baby home."
  News: Not guilty: State's case against Brower falls short — Judge Hale found Chester Brower, 30, not guilty of assault inflicting physical injury by strangulation after a bench trial, saying the State had not proven its case beyond a reasonable doubt. At about 1:09 a.m. on 10 March, Josefina Killian called 911 from a bathroom in the house on Fifth Street that she shared with Chester Brower, saying Brower had choked her in the kitchen. Opinions in the hallway afterward were as divided as the evidence. A relative of Josefina Killian, outside the courtroom: "We just want to be left alone now." ADA Deja Cisneros: "We presented the evidence we had. We accept the verdict." Cassandra Yamamoto: "Brower can finally go home." Brower had been held in the Hollis County Detention Center for 49 days.

=== story.sharedApartment ===

  Charges: Possession with Intent to Sell or Deliver (Schedule II); Possession of a Schedule II Controlled Substance; Maintaining a Dwelling for Controlled Substances; Possession of Drug Paraphernalia

  TEMPLATE story.sharedApartment
  Summary: On 21 April, narcotics detectives executed a search warrant at a three-bedroom apartment on Saint Agnes Way in Port Hollis, leased to Aaron Lovett and shared with two roommates. In a shoebox on the top shelf of the hall closet they found 31 knotted baggies of cocaine totaling 22.4 grams and $1,840 in cash; a digital scale was in a kitchen drawer. Lovett was the only person home. One roommate, Perla Crowley, who has a prior conviction for selling cocaine, moved out two days later. Lovett, a warehouse forklift operator, says the closet was shared and the box wasn't his.
  Verdicts: Possession with Intent to Sell or Deliver (Schedule II): guilty; Possession of a Schedule II Controlled Substance: guilty; Maintaining a Dwelling for Controlled Substances: guilty; Possession of Drug Paraphernalia: guilty

  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$1,900.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | State Crime Laboratory fee: US$600.00 | Supervision fees ($40/month): US$960.00 | Total: US$3,733.00

  Reaction: Angelo Calderon: "My client has a chance to put this behind them, and intends to take it."
  Reaction: A relative of Lovett's: "We're just grateful to have Lovett home tonight."
  News: Guilty: Lovett case ends in conviction — Aaron Lovett stood as Judge Hale announced the verdict: guilty on all 4 counts. The judge then imposed a suspended sentence and 24 months of probation. According to court records, on 21 April, narcotics detectives executed a search warrant at a three-bedroom apartment on Saint Agnes Way in Port Hollis, leased to Aaron Lovett and shared with two roommates. A relative of Lovett's: "We're just grateful to have Lovett home tonight." ADA Deja Cisneros: "The conviction matters. We'd hoped the sentence would say more about addiction." === story.caregiverAccount ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005050 · State of North Carolina v. Carmela Dimitriou
  Charges: Exploitation of an Older Adult; Exploitation of an Older Adult; Financial Transaction Card Fraud
  Defendant: Carmela Dimitriou, 34, Home health aide; priors: 0; Released on a written promise to appear, with electronic monitoring.
  TEMPLATE story.caregiverAccount
  Summary: For two years Carmela Dimitriou, a home health aide, cared for Sheila Coleridge, 91, a retired textile mill supervisor who lives alone in Wexley. Over fourteen months, $68,000 left Coleridge's savings: checks to Dimitriou written in her handwriting and signed by Coleridge, a used car titled to Dimitriou, and cash withdrawals made with Coleridge's debit card. Coleridge's niece, who lives in Ohio, found the statements after she fell and was hospitalized. Dimitriou says Coleridge insisted on every gift and called Dimitriou "the only family who shows up." Coleridge, who has mild dementia but sometimes recalls things clearly, has told different people different things.
  Verdicts: Exploitation of an Older Adult: guilty; Exploitation of an Older Adult: guilty; Financial Transaction Card Fraud: guilty
  [Counts 1, 2, 3 (consolidated): Exploitation of an Older Adult] Class G Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | Minimum 10, maximum 21 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 10 months. | Maximum term: 21 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Projected release: no earlier than 2 September 2027, and no later than 2 November 2027. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$68,000.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$4,200.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | Total: US$72,473.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: ADA Laurel Lancaster: "No verdict undoes the harm. But this one says it mattered."
  Reaction: Sofia Carver: "We respect the court, even though we see the evidence differently."
  Reaction: A family friend of the Coleridges, outside the courtroom: "Dimitriou will be out before we feel safe again."
  Reaction: A relative of Dimitriou's: "It's hard. But it could have been worse, and we know it."
  News: Carmela Dimitriou convicted in exploitation of an older adult case — Superior Court Judge Hale found Carmela Dimitriou, 34, guilty on all 3 counts, and imposed 10 to 21 months in prison, ending a case that began with an arrest in Wexley. For two years Carmela Dimitriou, a home health aide, cared for Sheila Coleridge, 91, a retired textile mill supervisor who lives alone in Wexley. Opinions in the hallway afterward were as divided as the evidence. ADA Laurel Lancaster: "No verdict undoes the harm. But this one says it mattered." A family friend of the Coleridges, outside the courtroom: "Dimitriou will be out before we feel safe again." Dimitriou was taken into custody in the courtroom. The judgment includes US$72,473.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

=== story.parkingLotShooting ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005060 · State of North Carolina v. Gilbert Tunstall
  Charges: Assault with a Deadly Weapon with Intent to Kill Inflicting Serious Injury
  Defendant: Gilbert Tunstall, 24, Electrician's apprentice; priors: 3; Released on a $200,000 secured bond; missed one court date early on, then was re-released.
  TEMPLATE story.parkingLotShooting
  Summary: At about 8:47 p.m. on 17 May, after a near-collision on Route 17, Gemma Nobles followed Gilbert Tunstall's pickup into the parking lot of the Dollar General on Route 17 in Bayard. Gemma Nobles got out and walked to Tunstall's driver's door. Tunstall, who has a concealed carry permit, fired twice through the open window, hitting Gemma Nobles in the shoulder and abdomen. Gemma Nobles survived after surgery. Tunstall says Gemma Nobles reached into the truck and grabbed Tunstall's collar; Gemma Nobles says she only knocked on the door frame and yelled. A store camera caught most of it from sixty yards away.
  Verdicts: Assault with a Deadly Weapon with Intent to Kill Inflicting Serious Injury: guilty
  Condition: no contact with the victim

Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Projected release: no earlier than 2 April 2033, and no later than 2 August 2034. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$9,000.00 | State Crime Laboratory fee: US$600.00 | Total: US$9,813.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The evidence was clear, and the court followed it."
  Reaction: Fiona Adcock: "We respect the court, even though we see the evidence differently."
  Reaction: A relative of Gemma Nobles, outside the courtroom: "It's not enough. It's never going to be enough."
  Reaction: A relative of Tunstall's: "We'll be waiting when it's over."
  News: Tunstall headed to prison after assault with a deadly weapon with intent to kill inflicting serious injury conviction — A Hollis County judge has convicted Gilbert Tunstall of assault with a deadly weapon with intent to kill inflicting serious injury, and imposed 77 to 105 months in prison. According to court records, at about 8:47 p.m. on 17 May, after a near-collision on Route 17, Gemma Nobles followed Gilbert Tunstall's pickup into the parking lot of the Dollar General on Route 17 in Bayard. Opinions in the hallway afterward were as divided as the evidence. A relative of Gemma Nobles, outside the courtroom: "It's not enough. It's never going to be enough." === story.contractorDeposits ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005070 · State of North Carolina v. Kai Stancil
  Charges: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses
  Defendant: Kai Stancil, 37, Remodeling contractor; priors: 0; Released on a $25,000 secured bond. Has made every court date.
  TEMPLATE story.contractorDeposits
  Summary: Over five months, Kai Stancil, owner of Pinecrest Remodeling in Cane Creek, took deposits from six homeowners for kitchen and bathroom remodels, $61,000 in all. None of the work was finished; three jobs never started. Clarissa Doby paid $14,500 after Stancil told Doby the cabinets were "already on order and arriving in three weeks." No cabinets were ever ordered. Stancil says the business collapsed when a supplier went under and he was robbing Peter to pay Paul to keep it alive, not stealing. The State charged each family's deposit as its own count; Doby's leads the indictment because of what Stancil said to get it.
  Verdicts: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: notGuilty; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty; Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty
  Continued mid-trial to about 23 Nov 2026

=== story.fishingCabin ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005080 · State of North Carolina v. Brent Haddad
  Charges: First-Degree Murder; Insurance Fraud
  Defendant: Brent Haddad, 59, Co-owner of a marine supply business; priors: 3; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $2,000,000 secured bond for 41 days.
  TEMPLATE story.fishingCabin
  Summary: Brent Haddad and Rhett Tyson owned Harbor Point Outboard together for eleven years. On 11 January, during a cold snap, the two drove to Tyson's cabin on Sutton Lake to hunt ducks. At about 7:14 a.m., Haddad called 911 to say the shotgun had gone off while he was unloading it on the porch and Tyson was dead. The medical examiner says the shot was fired from about four feet away. Three weeks earlier, Tyson had told a lawyer he wanted to force Haddad out of the business, and the company held a $500,000 life insurance policy on each partner payable to the other.
  Verdicts: First-Degree Murder: guilty; Insurance Fraud: guilty
  [Counts 1, 2 (consolidated): First-Degree Murder] Class A Felony, prior record level II | Life imprisonment without parole. Committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.

  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: ADA Eddie Bolton: "Our assistant district attorneys tried this case the right way, and the result speaks for itself."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "This sentence is far more than this case called for, and we'll be reviewing every option."
  Reaction: A member of the Tyson family, outside the courtroom: "Nothing brings back what we lost. But today someone was held responsible."
  Reaction: A former employer of Haddad's: "We'll be there every visiting day. Every one."
  News: Judge finds Haddad guilty of first-degree murder — Brent Haddad stood as Judge Hale announced the verdict: guilty on both counts. The judge then imposed life in prison without the possibility of parole. According to court records, Brent Haddad and Rhett Tyson owned Harbor Point Outboard together for eleven years. Two weeks later police arrested Miriam Craddock, who sold one of the stolen rifles. Miriam Craddock agreed to testify in exchange for a reduced charge and named Lester Godinez as the partner who carried the handgun. Godinez says Miriam Craddock is protecting the real accomplice.

Prosecutors said that at about 1:45 a.m. on 16 February, two masked people forced their way into the home of Olamide Gutierrez, 67, and his spouse on Pell Avenue in Bayard, held them at gunpoint, zip-tied their wrists, and took a gun safe's contents, jewelry, and $3,000 in cash. === story.protectiveOrder ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005100 · State of North Carolina v. Gene Fujita
  Charges: Assault with a Deadly Weapon Inflicting Serious Injury; Violation of a Domestic Violence Protective Order
  Defendant: Gene Fujita, 39, Barber; priors: 3; Released on a $150,000 secured bond. Has made every court date.
  TEMPLATE story.protectiveOrder
  Summary: Devon Walston held a domestic violence protective order against Gene Fujita, the other parent of their eight-year-old, that barred contact except for exchanges arranged through a relative. On 30 May, Fujita came to Walston's apartment on Marlowe Boulevard unannounced at about 6:40 p.m. to pick up the child's medicine and school clothes. Minutes later Walston called 911 with a deep cut across the forearm that took nineteen stitches and nerve repair. Walston says Fujita pulled a box cutter. Fujita says Walston grabbed the box cutter off the counter and Fujita was cut too trying to take it away.
  Verdicts: Assault with a Deadly Weapon Inflicting Serious Injury: guilty; Violation of a Domestic Violence Protective Order: guilty

=== story.textMessages ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005200 · State of North Carolina v. Eduardo Strickland
  Charges: Involuntary Manslaughter; Cyberstalking
  Defendant: Eduardo Strickland, 19, College freshman; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $25,000 secured bond for 51 days.
  TEMPLATE story.textMessages
  Summary: Maureen Starling, 18, died by suicide in Wexley on 19 January. Over the previous six weeks, Eduardo Strickland, Starling's partner of two years, sent hundreds of messages urging her to stop delaying and follow through, and was on a video call during the final hour without calling for help. Earlier in the relationship, Strickland had repeatedly urged Starling to get treatment. The State says Strickland's pressure was the cause, and that the failure to call for help was culpable negligence. The defense says Starling had struggled for years, that words alone aren't a cause of death, and that Strickland was a teenager in crisis too.
  Verdicts: Involuntary Manslaughter: notGuilty; Cyberstalking: guilty

  TEMPLATE story.nightNurse
  Summary: Over seven weeks on the night shift of the cardiac step-down unit at Hollis Regional Medical Center, four patients who were not diabetic crashed with dangerously low blood sugar between 2 and 5 a.m. Three died: Fatima Walden, 71; Doris Kemp, 64; and Clarence Batts, 80. Lab tests on stored blood showed high insulin with almost no C-peptide, a sign the insulin came from a needle, not the body. Samantha Ezzell, a registered nurse, worked every one of those nights and had pulled insulin from the medication cabinet for patients who didn't need it. Ezzell says the cabinet log is unreliable, that other nurses used each other's badges, and that very sick elderly patients sometimes crash for no clear reason.

Both had zip-tie marks on their wrists. The electronic logging device in Ramon Coleridge's tractor-trailer puts his truck at both stops within an hour of each woman's last sighting. A zip tie of the same brand and an earring like one Talton wore were found in Coleridge's sleeper cab. Coleridge has a prior conviction for assault on a female. Coleridge says thousands of trucks pass through those stops every day, that the zip ties are for securing loads, and that the earring was left by a different hitchhiker he gave a ride.

TRIAL 26 CRS 005850 · State of North Carolina v. Amber Hollowell

  TEMPLATE story.eyeDropPoisoning
  Summary: On 2 June, Amber Hollowell called 911 to report that her husband, Montgomery Silva, had fallen down the stairs of their home in Wexley. Paramedics found Silva dead at the bottom of the steps. The medical examiner first called it an accident. A routine toxicology screen, sent out because Silva had a heart condition, came back with tetrahydrozoline at many times a therapeutic level, the decongestant in over-the-counter eye drops. Swallowed, it slows the heart and drops blood pressure. Over the next month, Hollowell wrote herself $41,000 in checks from Silva's account and filed a life insurance claim. Hollowell first told police Silva had been depressed, then later said she had put drops in his water over three days to make him sick, not to kill him.
  Verdicts: First-Degree Murder: notGuilty; Forgery of an Instrument: guilty; Uttering a Forged Instrument: notGuilty; Insurance Fraud: guilty

  [Prayer for judgment continued] Prayer for judgment is continued. No sentence is imposed. | The finding of guilt remains and will count as a prior conviction.

  Summary: On 19 February, Vaughn Duquesne, a media executive, and his wife, Joanne, were shot to death while watching television in the den of their Wexley home. Vaughn was shot five times, once in the back of the head at close range; Joanne was shot ten times, the last while crawling. Their sons, Arlo Duquesne and Grant, called 911 that night, sobbing, saying they had come home from a movie and found them. Over the next six months the brothers spent nearly $700,000: watches, a restaurant, a Porsche, tennis coaching. Two days before the killings they had bought two shotguns in another county using a stolen driver's license. Grant confessed to a therapist, who told his lover, who told police. Duquesne now says the brothers killed their parents out of fear, after years of sexual abuse by their father, because they believed their parents were about to kill them.
  Verdicts: First-Degree Murder: guilty; First-Degree Murder: notGuilty; Identity Theft: notGuilty; Obstruction of Justice: guilty

More than 200 people invested $14 million: retirees, church members, the volunteer fire department pension board. There was never a fund. Statements were made on Aycock's office printer. Old investors were paid with new investors' money, and about $4 million went to Aycock's lake house, private school tuition, and a failing car dealership. The scheme collapsed on 18 December when the fire department board asked to withdraw its money and there was nothing to give. Kirsten Vestal, 81, lost everything she had. Aycock says he always intended to make it right and was a victim of the market.

The dispatcher told Smallwood "we don't need you to do that" when he said he was following. Minutes later, Smallwood and Renfro were in a fight on the grass behind the townhouses. Neighbors heard screams for help, then one shot. Renfro was dead. Smallwood had a bloody nose and cuts on the back of his head. He says Renfro jumped him, was banging his head on the sidewalk, and reached for his gun.

=== story.trailMurders ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 005920 · State of North Carolina v. Thaddeus Faulk
  Charges: First-Degree Murder; First-Degree Murder; First-Degree Kidnapping; First-Degree Kidnapping
  Defendant: Thaddeus Faulk, 50, Pharmacy technician; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $1,500,000 secured bond for 47 days.
  TEMPLATE story.trailMurders
  Summary: On 12 September, Angelica Sherrill, 13, and Liberty Carr, 14, were dropped off at the Creek Trail in Port Hollis on a school holiday. They walked to the old railroad bridge. Sherrill recorded 43 seconds on her phone: a man in a blue jacket and jeans walking toward them on the bridge, and a man's voice saying, "Guys... down the hill." Their bodies were found the next day in the woods across the creek, throats cut. The case went unsolved for five years. Then a records clerk found a tip from the first week: Thaddeus Faulk, a local pharmacy technician, had told an officer he was on the trail that afternoon wearing a blue jacket. An unspent .40-caliber round found between the girls' bodies was matched to a pistol in Faulk's home. In jail, Faulk confessed more than 60 times to guards, a psychologist, and his spouse on the phone. Faulk says the confessions came from a breakdown in solitary confinement and that the killings were done by others.
  Verdicts: First-Degree Murder: notGuilty; First-Degree Murder: guilty; First-Degree Kidnapping: guilty; First-Degree Kidnapping: notGuilty

  TEMPLATE story.jailSmuggling
  Summary: On 19 February, Caridad Burkhart, 31, was found unresponsive in a cell at the Hollis County Detention Center, where she was being held on a probation violation. She died of fentanyl toxicity. Two other inmates in the same pod overdosed that week and survived. Investigators traced the drugs to letters soaked in fentanyl and to small balloons. Jail phone calls in code and a cash app account led to Kenya Aguilar, a detention officer in Pod C. A search of Aguilar's car in the staff lot found 12 grams of fentanyl. An inmate who was the go-between says Aguilar was paid $2,000 a trip by an outside dealer. The logs show Aguilar made cell checks every 30 minutes the night Burkhart died; video shows she never left the officer's desk.

The mechanic plugged in a scanner: the transmission computer recorded 164,000 miles. The odometer read 68,000. A DMV investigation found 41 cars sold from Murillo's lot over two years with mileage dropped between 40,000 and 110,000 miles, using a $300 handheld device bought online. Many of the cars had been bought at auctions with their true mileage printed on the auction sheets. Murillo says she buys cars as-is, never touched an odometer, and that the cars must have been tampered with before she got them.

He said they had been drinking wine by the pool and he found her when he came inside. The medical examiner ruled it a homicide by blunt force. Investigators found that Pittman had been exchanging emails with a male escort, and that Dudley's company was about to lay her off, which would have cost her a large life insurance policy.

TRIAL 26 CRS 006090 · State of North Carolina v. Gilbert Sauls
  Charges: First-Degree Murder
  Defendant: Gilbert Sauls, 84, Retired tobacco farmer; priors: 5; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $500,000 secured bond for 109 days.
  TEMPLATE story.mercyKilling
  Summary: On 24 December, Gilbert Sauls, 84, called 911 from the farmhouse in Bayard where he had lived with his wife, Jacinta Aycock, for sixty-one years. "I've shot my wife," he told the dispatcher. "I'll be on the porch." Aycock, 81, had advanced Alzheimer's. She no longer knew him, screamed through the nights, and had been refused by two memory care facilities after striking aides. Their savings were gone. Sauls told deputies that years earlier, when she was first diagnosed, she made him promise he would never let her "end up like that." He wrote a note, laid out his best suit, and shot her once while she slept. He had intended to shoot himself afterward but couldn't. The district attorney charged first-degree murder, saying the law doesn't allow anyone to decide another person's life is not worth living.

Magnolia Callahan, 77, gave him $14,000, nearly all of her insurance check. Almost no work was done. His contractor license was a photocopy of someone else's with the name changed. On 17 January, Farah Rollins was hospitalized with a severe infection in both knees after injections; Glenda Hardee was treated for sepsis a week later. Lab testing found the vials contained unsterile cord blood product from an overseas website. Four minutes later it hit Kelsey Renfro's sedan head-on at a combined speed of about 130 mph. Renfro and her daughter, Maya, 9, died at the scene. Lonzo Cannady survived with a broken pelvis. His blood alcohol was 0.25 at the hospital. His license had been revoked after his third impaired driving conviction, and a court had ordered an ignition interlock device; the pickup belonged to a friend and had none. Because of his prior convictions and the circumstances, the district attorney charged second-degree murder, which requires malice.
  Verdicts: Second-Degree Murder: guilty; Second-Degree Murder: guilty; Felony Death by Vehicle: guilty; Habitual Impaired Driving: guilty; Driving While License Revoked for Impaired Driving: guilty
  [Counts 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 (consolidated): Second-Degree Murder] Class B1 Felony, prior record level IV | Presumptive range | Minimum 292, maximum 363 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 292 months (24 yr 4 mo). | Maximum term: 363 months.   [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$10,000.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$4,500.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | State Crime Laboratory fee: US$600.00 | Total: US$15,373.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).

TRIAL 26 CRS 006190 · State of North Carolina v. Dustin Bhatt
  Charges: Felony Larceny; Involuntary Manslaughter; Injury to Real Property
  Defendant: Dustin Bhatt, 35, Scrap metal collector; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $40,000 secured bond for 17 days.
  TEMPLATE story.copperTheft
  Summary: Just after midnight on 2 May, Dustin Bhatt and Herbert Asante cut through the fence of a power substation on the edge of Dunmore Mills with bolt cutters to steal copper grounding wire. Bhatt, who had worked as an electrician's helper, told Asante which wires were "dead." One wasn't. Asante was electrocuted when he cut a cable connected to an energized transformer. The arc flash knocked out power to 4,000 homes, including a nursing home on oxygen machines. Bhatt dragged Asante to the truck, drove him to the hospital, and left before police arrived. Investigators found 300 pounds of copper in the truck bed and Bhatt's fingerprints on the bolt cutters. Bhatt admits the theft but says Asante chose to come and chose the wire.

  Summary: A hailstorm crossed Dunmore Mills on 5 March. Three days later, Savannah McDuffie filed a claim for $23,600 to replace the roof of her house on Railroad Avenue. An engineer hired by the insurer says the marks match a ball-peen hammer. The National Weather Service logged pea- to nickel-sized hail in Dunmore Mills that night, and two neighbors' minor claims were paid. A neighbor saw McDuffie on the roof with a hammer two days after the storm. McDuffie says she was nailing down a lifted ridge cap, and that a twenty-year-old roof bruises easily.
  Verdicts: Insurance Fraud: guilty
  [Count 1: Insurance Fraud] Class H Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | 5 to 15 months, suspended. Community punishment: 24 months of probation.
  [Conditions of probation] Regular conditions of probation under G.S. 15A-1343(b) apply. | Supervised probation: report as directed, submit to warrantless searches by the probation officer, remain within the jurisdiction.
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$23,600.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$2,900.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | Supervision fees ($40/month): US$960.00 | Total: US$27,733.00

  TEMPLATE story.receiptCheck
  Summary: On 4 March at about 5:40 p.m., Latrice Canady bought a 65-inch television at register 7 at Harbor Mart. The card reader beeped, but the receipt printer jammed and nothing came out. Canady, due at work at six, pushed the cart toward the exit. A loss prevention associate, Corey Lassiter, stepped in front of the cart, took hold of it, and said, "No receipt, no TV. We're not doing this today." Canady said she had paid and was late, and started recording on her phone. Deputy Shane Ivey of the Hollis County Sheriff's Office, working an off-duty shift at the store, came over. When Canady asked whether the law required her to show a receipt, the officer said, "Yes. It's store policy, and you're on store property." As Canady kept recording, the officer took the phone out of her hand and tried to stop the recording, and Canady pulled her arm back. Canady was handcuffed and charged with felony larceny and resisting an officer. The store's register journal shows no completed sale at register 7 at that time. Canady's bank app shows a pending $1,184.33 charge from Harbor Mart that afternoon.

  Charges: Assault with a Deadly Weapon with Intent to Kill Inflicting Serious Injury
  Defendant: Eloise Puckett, 39, Licensed security guard; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $125,000 secured bond for 36 days.
  TEMPLATE story.securityGuardShot
  Summary: Shortly before closing on 16 March, Eloise Puckett, the armed security guard at Rooster's on Main, put Corina Duong out the door after Corina Duong threw a drink at a bartender. Ten minutes later Corina Duong came back up the sidewalk toward the door, shouting, with something dark in her right hand. Eloise Puckett drew and fired three times. One round hit Corina Duong in the hip. The object was a phone. Puckett says Corina Duong had told her on the way out, "I've got something in the car for you," and came back holding the dark object low like a pistol. The bar's door camera shows the approach but not Corina Duong's hand clearly.
  Verdicts: Assault with a Deadly Weapon with Intent to Kill Inflicting Serious Injury: notGuilty
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The court has spoken. Our office will keep standing up for violence in our community."
  Reaction: Keziah Hobgood: "Puckett has lived under this charge for a long time. It's over."
  Reaction: Corina Duong's sister, outside the courtroom: "We just want to be left alone now."
  Reaction: A relative of Puckett's: "I knew it. I knew the truth would come out."
  News: Acquittal in ‘Bouncer Says He Saw a Gun; Patron Was Holding a Phone’ case — The State's case against Eloise Puckett ended in acquittal in a case that began in Wexley. According to court records, shortly before closing on 16 March, Eloise Puckett, the armed security guard at Rooster's on Main, put Corina Duong out the door after Corina Duong threw a drink at a bartender. Reaction outside the courthouse was split. District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The court has spoken. Our office will keep standing up for violence in our community." Keziah Hobgood: "Puckett has lived under this charge for a long time. It's over." Corina Duong's sister, outside the courtroom: "We just want to be left alone now." Puckett had been held in the Hollis County Detention Center for 36 days.

=== story.parkingDeckRobbery ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006730 · State of North Carolina v. Clark Bhatt
  Charges: Robbery with a Firearm or Other Dangerous Weapon; Financial Transaction Card Fraud
  Defendant: Clark Bhatt, 29, Unemployed; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $75,000 secured bond for 37 days.
  TEMPLATE story.parkingDeckRobbery
  Summary: At 7:25 a.m. on 15 January, Edith Jeffreys, a nurse coming off a twelve-hour night shift at Hollis Regional Medical Center, was unlocking her car on level 3 of the parking deck when a man in a gray hoodie held a folding knife to her side and took her purse. Forty minutes later Edith Jeffreys's debit card bought $84 in gas and cigarettes at Pell Avenue Quik Stop. The station camera shows Clark Bhatt. Two days later Edith Jeffreys picked Clark Bhatt from a photo array as the man with the knife. Bhatt says a man he knows only as "Junior" gave him the card to buy gas and he was nowhere near the hospital.
  Verdicts: Robbery with a Firearm or Other Dangerous Weapon: notGuilty; Financial Transaction Card Fraud: guilty
  Stay-away order: 2 years
  [Count 2: Financial Transaction Card Fraud] Class I Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | 4 to 14 months, suspended. Community punishment: 24 months of probation.
  [Conditions of probation] Regular conditions of probation under G.S. 15A-1343(b) apply. | Supervised probation: report as directed, submit to warrantless searches by the probation officer, remain within the jurisdiction.
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$900.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$2,200.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | Supervision fees ($40/month): US$960.00 | Total: US$4,333.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: ADA Laurel Lancaster: "No verdict undoes the harm. But this one says it mattered."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "Probation is hard. Bhatt knows that, and knows what's at stake."
  Reaction: Edith Jeffreys's sister, outside the courtroom: "Probation. I don't know what to say."
  News: Split verdict for Bhatt: guilty on 1 of 2 counts — Superior Court Judge Hale found Clark Bhatt, 29, guilty on 1 of 2 counts, and imposed a suspended sentence and 24 months of probation, ending a case that began with an arrest in Bayard. Prosecutors said that at 7:25 a.m. on 15 January, Edith Jeffreys, a nurse coming off a twelve-hour night shift at Hollis Regional Medical Center, was unlocking her car on level 3 of the parking deck when a man in a gray hoodie held a folding knife to her side and took her purse. Outside, a small group said probation wasn't justice. Edith Jeffreys's sister, outside the courtroom: "Probation. I don't know what to say." Cassandra Yamamoto: "Probation is hard. Bhatt knows that, and knows what's at stake." Bhatt left the courthouse with family. The judgment includes US$4,333.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

=== story.mailedPills ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006740 · State of North Carolina v. Darnell Brannon
  Charges: Trafficking in Opium, Heroin, or Fentanyl (4–14 grams)
  Defendant: Darnell Brannon, 35, Delivery driver; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $40,000 secured bond for 48 days.
  TEMPLATE story.mailedPills
  Summary: A postal inspector in Charlotte flagged a padded envelope bound for Box 214 at the Bayard post office: a handwritten return address that didn't exist, heavy tape, and postage paid in cash. A drug dog alerted, and a federal magistrate signed a warrant. Inside, wrapped in a child's coloring book, were 100 blue pills stamped "M30," which the lab found contained 9.6 grams of fentanyl mixture. Inspectors resealed it and watched the box. On 10 February, Darnell Brannon opened Box 214 with a key, took the envelope, and was arrested in the parking lot. The box was rented under the name "Jay Moore" with a fake ID. Brannon says a friend paid him $50 to pick up "a package" and he had no idea what was inside.
  Verdicts: Trafficking in Opium, Heroin, or Fentanyl (4–14 grams): guilty
  [Count 1: Trafficking in Opium, Heroin, or Fentanyl (4–14 grams)] Class F Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | Minimum 13, maximum 25 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 13 months (1 yr 1 mo). | Maximum term: 25 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Credit for 48 days already served in pretrial confinement (G.S. 15-196.1). | Projected release: no earlier than 15 October 2027, and no later than 14 January 2028. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$3,800.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | State Crime Laboratory fee: US$600.00 | Total: US$4,673.00
  Reaction: ADA Deja Cisneros: "No verdict undoes the harm. But this one says it mattered."
  Reaction: Angelo Calderon: "We're disappointed, but the court listened. Brannon will do the time and come home."
  News: Guilty: Brannon case ends in conviction — Superior Court Judge Hale found Darnell Brannon, 35, guilty, and imposed 13 to 25 months in prison, ending a case that began with an arrest in Wexley. According to court records, a postal inspector in Charlotte flagged a padded envelope bound for Box 214 at the Bayard post office: a handwritten return address that didn't exist, heavy tape, and postage paid in cash. Angelo Calderon: "We're disappointed, but the court listened. Brannon will do the time and come home." ADA Deja Cisneros: "No verdict undoes the harm. But this one says it mattered." Brannon was taken into custody in the courtroom. Counting 48 days already served, the earliest possible release is 15 October 2027. The judgment includes US$4,673.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

=== story.crossingGuardCrash ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006750 · State of North Carolina v. Joseph Salazar
  Charges: Felony Hit and Run (Serious Injury or Death); Misdemeanor Death by Vehicle
  Defendant: Joseph Salazar, 35, Contractor; priors: 10; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $25,000 secured bond for 44 days.
  TEMPLATE story.crossingGuardCrash
  Summary: At 7:48 a.m. on 18 May, Tasha Matthis, 68, a crossing guard for twelve years at Bayard County High, stepped into the marked crosswalk with a stop sign raised. A silver SUV coming out of the sun hit Tasha Matthis and kept going. Tasha Matthis died at the hospital that afternoon. Two parents gave partial plates. That evening a trooper found Joseph Salazar's SUV in his garage with a cracked headlight, a dented hood, and Tasha Matthis's orange vest fibers in the grille. Salazar says he was blinded by the sun, felt a hit, thought he had struck the sign on the curb, and kept driving to a client meeting. He didn't learn a person had been hit until the trooper came to the door.
  Verdicts: Felony Hit and Run (Serious Injury or Death): notGuilty; Misdemeanor Death by Vehicle: notGuilty
  Reaction: ADA Deja Cisneros: "A bench trial is a judge's call to make. We made our case and we'll move on to the next one."
  Reaction: Sofia Carver: "Suspicion isn't proof. Today the system worked."
  Reaction: A family friend of the Matthiss, outside the courtroom: "We'll be living with this. Salazar gets to walk away."
  News: Acquittal in ‘Driver Charged in Hit-and-Run Death of Crossing Guard’ case — Joseph Salazar left the Hollis County Courthouse a free defendant after Judge Hale returned a verdict of not guilty. According to court records, at 7:48 a.m. on 18 May, Tasha Matthis, 68, a crossing guard for twelve years at Bayard County High, stepped into the marked crosswalk with a stop sign raised. Someone in the back row shouted as the verdict was read. ADA Deja Cisneros: "A bench trial is a judge's call to make. We made our case and we'll move on to the next one." A family friend of the Matthiss, outside the courtroom: "We'll be living with this. Salazar gets to walk away." Sofia Carver: "Suspicion isn't proof. Today the system worked." Salazar had been held in the Hollis County Detention Center for 44 days.

=== story.mobileHomeSale ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006760 · State of North Carolina v. Malcolm Vann
  Charges: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses
  Defendant: Malcolm Vann, 51, Used car salesman; priors: 8; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $8,000 secured bond for 39 days.
  TEMPLATE story.mobileHomeSale
  Summary: On 17 April, Joanna Hartsell paid Malcolm Vann $18,500 in cash for a 2004 single-wide at Lot 9 of Pine Haven Mobile Home Park, saved over three years. Malcolm Vann handed over a bill of sale and said the title would come in the mail. It never did. Two months later a bank repossessed the home: the title was in Malcolm Vann's late mother's name, with a lien of $22,000 that hadn't been paid in a year. Records show Malcolm Vann had sold the same home a year earlier to another buyer, who backed out and got half the money back. Vann says he was the heir, believed he could sell it, and spent the money paying down the lien before the bank "jumped the gun."
  Verdicts: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: notGuilty
  Reaction: ADA Deja Cisneros: "We presented the evidence we had. We accept the verdict."
  Reaction: Angelo Calderon: "Vann can finally go home."
  Reaction: A family friend of the Hartsells, outside the courtroom: "We never wanted this case. Maybe now everyone can heal."
  News: Not guilty: State's case against Vann falls short — In a ruling from the bench, Judge Hale acquitted Vann of obtaining property by false pretenses. On 17 April, Joanna Hartsell paid Malcolm Vann $18,500 in cash for a 2004 single-wide at Lot 9 of Pine Haven Mobile Home Park, saved over three years. Supporters hugged on the courthouse steps. A family friend of the Hartsells, outside the courtroom: "We never wanted this case. Maybe now everyone can heal." Angelo Calderon: "Vann can finally go home." ADA Deja Cisneros: "We presented the evidence we had. We accept the verdict." Vann had been held in the Hollis County Detention Center for 39 days.

=== story.fakeFundraiser ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006770 · State of North Carolina v. Anika Whitfield
  Charges: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses
  Defendant: Anika Whitfield, 48, Salon owner; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $8,000 secured bond for 30 days.
  TEMPLATE story.fakeFundraiser
  Summary: After a house fire in Cane Creek left a family of five with nothing, Anika Whitfield set up an online fundraiser with photos of the burned house and organized a pancake breakfast at the fire hall on 28 February. Donors gave $7,400 in all. Adelaide Emerson, a retired teacher, gave $500. The family received $600 in gift cards and was told the rest "went to costs." Bank records show the online money and the breakfast cash went to Anika Whitfield's personal account, then to her rent, a car repair, and a beach weekend. Whitfield says she was holding the rest for a deposit on a rental house for the family and spent some by mistake from the same account.
  Verdicts: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty
  Form of judgment: conditional discharge
  [Conditional discharge (G.S. 15A-1341(a4))] Further proceedings are deferred without entry of judgment. | The defendant is placed on 12 months of supervised probation. On successful completion, the charge will be dismissed.
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$500.00 | Supervision fees ($40/month): US$480.00 | Total: US$1,193.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The conviction matters. We'd hoped the sentence would say more about financial crime."
  Reaction: Fiona Adcock: "Whitfield gets to keep the job at self-employed. That matters more than people think."
  Reaction: Adelaide Emerson's father, outside the courtroom: "We'll be at every hearing if Whitfield slips once."
  Reaction: A former employer of Whitfield's: "We're just grateful to have Whitfield home tonight."
  News: Judge finds Whitfield guilty of obtaining property by false pretenses — Anika Whitfield stood as Judge Hale announced the verdict: guilty. The judge then imposed a conditional discharge: if Whitfield completes 12 months of probation, the charge will be dismissed. After a house fire in Cane Creek left a family of five with nothing, Anika Whitfield set up an online fundraiser with photos of the burned house and organized a pancake breakfast at the fire hall on 28 February. Some in the community questioned whether the sentence was enough. Adelaide Emerson's father, outside the courtroom: "We'll be at every hearing if Whitfield slips once." District Attorney Mei Sloan: "The conviction matters. We'd hoped the sentence would say more about financial crime." Whitfield left the courthouse with family. The judgment includes US$1,193.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution. Courthouse observers said parts of the sentence appear to depart from North Carolina's sentencing laws.

The pharmacist called the office. The dentist had never seen the patient. The state's prescription database showed six such scripts on the same dentist's pad in two months, at four pharmacies, for four different patient names, all picked up by a person who matched Gemma Futrell, a receptionist at the practice. Pharmacy cameras at two stores show Gemma Futrell. Futrell says a former coworker forged them and asked her to pick them up for "family members who couldn't drive."

  Reaction: ADA Gwen Bethea: "No verdict undoes the harm. But this one says it mattered."

  Reaction: A relative of Futrell's: "This is the last time. It has to be."
  News: Futrell convicted, avoids prison — Superior Court Judge Hale found Gemma Futrell, 26, guilty on both counts, and imposed a suspended sentence and 24 months of probation, ending a case that began with an arrest in Dunmore Mills. According to court records, a pharmacist at Main Street Drug noticed something wrong on 13 April: a prescription for 30 oxycodone tablets from a dentist, for a patient who'd supposedly had a root canal, written in a dentist's name for a dose dentists almost never write. === story.fosterChildInjury ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006790 · State of North Carolina v. Jade Ramsey
  Charges: Felony Child Abuse Inflicting Serious Physical Injury
  Defendant: Jade Ramsey, 36, Long-haul trucker; priors: 0; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $300,000 secured bond for 84 days.
  TEMPLATE story.fosterChildInjury
  Summary: Janice Cogdell, three, had lived with Jade Ramsey as a foster child for five months when Jade Ramsey brought her to the emergency room at 8:10 p.m. on 15 January with a swollen left arm. X-rays showed a spiral fracture of the upper arm. Jade Ramsey said Janice Cogdell had fallen from the top bunk while playing with the older foster child, who is seven. The child abuse pediatrician who examined Janice Cogdell found four bruises on the upper arm in the shape of fingertips, and says a spiral fracture in a three-year-old comes from twisting, not a fall. The defense's expert, a biomechanical engineer, says a child who catches an arm in a bunk bed ladder while falling can twist it the same way. The older foster child told a social worker "she fell," then later, "I don't know."
  Verdicts: Felony Child Abuse Inflicting Serious Physical Injury: guilty
  [Count 1: Felony Child Abuse Inflicting Serious Physical Injury] Class D Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | Minimum 51, maximum 74 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 51 months (4 yr 3 mo). | Maximum term: 74 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Credit for 84 days already served in pretrial confinement (G.S. 15-196.1). | Projected release: no earlier than 10 November 2030, and no later than 10 October 2031. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$1,500.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$2,500.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | Total: US$4,273.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "We're grateful to the officers and witnesses who saw this case through."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "We respect the court, even though we see the evidence differently."
  Reaction: Janice Cogdell's sister, outside the courtroom: "It's not enough. It's never going to be enough."
  News: Judge finds Ramsey guilty of felony child abuse inflicting serious physical injury — After hearing the evidence without a jury, Judge Hale found Ramsey guilty, and imposed 51 to 74 months in prison. Janice Cogdell, three, had lived with Jade Ramsey as a foster child for five months when Jade Ramsey brought her to the emergency room at 8:10 p.m. on 15 January with a swollen left arm. Reaction outside the courthouse was split. Cassandra Yamamoto: "We respect the court, even though we see the evidence differently." Janice Cogdell's sister, outside the courtroom: "It's not enough. It's never going to be enough." District Attorney Mei Sloan: "We're grateful to the officers and witnesses who saw this case through." Ramsey was taken into custody in the courtroom. Counting 84 days already served, the earliest possible release is 10 November 2030. The judgment includes US$4,273.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

In a shed they found a carpet-covered treadmill, a "break stick" used to pry dogs' jaws apart, injectable antibiotics, and a notebook of weights and dates. A shallow grave held two dogs with fresh wounds. Tyson says he breeds and sells dogs, the scars come from fights when dogs got loose, and a man who rented the back acre used the shed.

=== story.hotelPartyOverdose ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006820 · State of North Carolina v. Alice McCall
  Charges: Death by Distribution
  Defendant: Alice McCall, 19, Line cook; priors: 2; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $125,000 secured bond for 37 days.
  TEMPLATE story.hotelPartyOverdose
  Summary: Clyde Achterberg, 20, was found unresponsive in Room 112 at Harbor Motor Inn at 6:15 a.m., the morning after a birthday party on 14 January that broke up around 2 a.m. The medical examiner found a fatal level of fentanyl. Texts show Clyde Achterberg asked Alice McCall for "two blues" at 11:48 p.m., and Alice McCall answered "got u." At 12:20 a.m., Clyde Achterberg sent Alice McCall $20 on a payment app with the note "gas." McCall admits giving Clyde Achterberg two pills from her own supply, but says she never sold anything: the $20 was for driving Clyde Achterberg to the party, and she took the same pills that night. The law requires a sale.
  Verdicts: Death by Distribution: lesser
  Continued mid-trial to about 23 Nov 2026

TRIAL 26 CRS 006830 · State of North Carolina v. Lewis Peebles
  Charges: Exploitation of an Older Adult; Felony Larceny
  Defendant: Lewis Peebles, 26, Unemployed; priors: 6; Released on a $15,000 secured bond; missed one court date early on, then was re-released.
  TEMPLATE story.weddingRingPawn
  Summary: Lewis Peebles moved in with his grandmother, Toni Farooq, 78, a widow, after losing his apartment. On 9 March, Lewis Peebles pawned Toni Farooq's diamond wedding ring and his late grandfather's gold watch at Cash Corner Pawn for $650, under his own name. Toni Farooq's daughter noticed the ring missing from Toni Farooq's hand a week later. Toni Farooq first told a deputy she had never let the ring out of her sight; at a second interview she said she may have told Lewis Peebles to "take what you need." Toni Farooq's doctor says she has early memory loss.
  Verdicts: Exploitation of an Older Adult: notGuilty; Felony Larceny: notGuilty

  Reaction: Peebles's grandmother: "I'm taking my baby home."
  News: Judge acquits Peebles in exploitation of an older adult case — Judge Hale found Lewis Peebles, 26, not guilty of exploitation of an older adult after a bench trial, saying the State had not proven its case beyond a reasonable doubt. Lewis Peebles moved in with his grandmother, Toni Farooq, 78, a widow, after losing his apartment. Supporters hugged on the courthouse steps. A relative of Toni Farooq, outside the courtroom: "We just want to be left alone now." ADA Deja Cisneros: "We respect the court's decision, though we see the evidence differently." The case was heard over 3 day.

Now." Olive Lennon handed over $1,840. Deputies stopped Nayeli Stroud eleven minutes later two miles away and found $1,790 in a sock in the console and a black pistol under the seat. The pistol is a Daisy BB pistol made to look like a Glock, with the orange tip painted black. Stroud admits nothing. The defense says a BB pistol, unloaded when found, isn't a dangerous weapon, and the State has to prove Olive Lennon's life was actually endangered.

Now." Olive Lennon handed over $1,840. Several people walked out before the judge finished. A former employer of Stroud's: "Thank God. Thank God." === story.tobaccoBarnFire ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006850 · State of North Carolina v. Bernard Faircloth
  Charges: Burning of Certain Buildings
  Defendant: Bernard Faircloth, 40, Farmer; priors: 0; Released on a written promise to appear, with electronic monitoring.
  TEMPLATE story.tobaccoBarnFire
  Summary: A 1920s log tobacco barn on Bernard Faircloth's farm outside Dunmore Mills burned to the ground around 2 a.m. on 28 May, along with a tractor and a hay baler inside. Six weeks earlier Bernard Faircloth had raised the barn's insurance from $40,000 to $185,000, listing it as "historic." The farm was three payments behind on its equipment loan. The State fire investigator found a pour pattern across the dirt floor and a gas can melted near the doorway, and a lab found gasoline in the soil samples. Faircloth says the barn had a wood stove for curing and an old wiring run, that the tractor always leaked fuel, and that he was asleep at home a mile away when the fire started.
  Verdicts: Burning of Certain Buildings: guilty
  [Count 1: Burning of Certain Buildings] Class F Felony, prior record level I | Presumptive range | Minimum 13, maximum 25 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 13 months (1 yr 1 mo). | Maximum term: 25 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Projected release: no earlier than 2 December 2027, and no later than 2 March 2028. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$600.00 | State Crime Laboratory fee: US$600.00 | Total: US$1,413.00
  Reaction: ADA Deja Cisneros: "We're grateful to the officers and witnesses who saw this case through."
  Reaction: Beth Broadway: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Faircloth about next steps."
  News: Guilty: Faircloth case ends in conviction — Superior Court Judge Hale found Bernard Faircloth, 40, guilty, and imposed 13 to 25 months in prison, ending a case that began with an arrest in Dunmore Mills. According to court records, a 1920s log tobacco barn on Bernard Faircloth's farm outside Dunmore Mills burned to the ground around 2 a.m. on 28 May, along with a tractor and a hay baler inside. Opinions in the hallway afterward were as divided as the evidence. Beth Broadway: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Faircloth about next steps." ADA Deja Cisneros: "We're grateful to the officers and witnesses who saw this case through." Faircloth was taken into custody in the courtroom. The judgment includes US$1,413.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

=== story.fireDeptFunds ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006860 · State of North Carolina v. Edwin Bost
  Charges: Embezzlement
  Defendant: Edwin Bost, 36, School bookkeeper; priors: 17; Held in the Hollis County jail on a $11,000 secured bond for 52 days.
  TEMPLATE story.fireDeptFunds
  Summary: Edwin Bost kept the books for the Cane Creek Volunteer Fire Department for nine years, signing checks and depositing fish fry, raffle, and county money. When the department's new chief tried to buy a $12,000 set of turnout gear in the spring, the bank said the account held $814. An audit found $63,400 missing over fourteen months: cash from fundraisers never deposited and 41 checks written to Bost marked "reimbursement" with no receipts. Casino records show Bost spent $58,000 at a casino in Cherokee over the same months. Bost says the chief and the board approved every reimbursement, that much of the money paid for supplies he bought with his own card, and that the department's records were a shoebox mess long before he took over.
  Verdicts: Embezzlement: notGuilty
  Reaction: ADA Gwen Bethea: "We'll be talking with the investigators about what we could have done differently."
  Reaction: Angelo Calderon: "Bost can finally go home."
  News: Edwin Bost found not guilty of embezzlement — Judge Hale found Edwin Bost, 36, not guilty of embezzlement after a bench trial, saying the State had not proven its case beyond a reasonable doubt. Prosecutors said that Edwin Bost kept the books for the Cane Creek Volunteer Fire Department for nine years, signing checks and depositing fish fry, raffle, and county money. Someone in the back row shouted as the verdict was read. ADA Gwen Bethea: "We'll be talking with the investigators about what we could have done differently." Angelo Calderon: "Bost can finally go home." Bost had been held in the Hollis County Detention Center for 52 days.

=== story.cattleAuction ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006870 · State of North Carolina v. Yasmin Bryson
  Charges: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses
  Defendant: Yasmin Bryson, 40, Farmhand; priors: 6; Released on a $15,000 secured bond; missed one court date early on, then was re-released.
  TEMPLATE story.cattleAuction
  Summary: Allison Beale leased pasture next to Yasmin Bryson's land and ran 40 head of black Angus there. On 4 October, Yasmin Bryson hauled 22 of them to the Hollis Livestock Market and sold them in the Tuesday sale for $26,950, signing a form swearing the cattle were her. Allison Beale noticed the herd short a week later. The cattle carried Allison Beale's ear tags, and the market's video shows Yasmin Bryson unloading them. Bryson says Allison Beale owed her $31,000 for two years of hay, fencing, and feeding the cattle through a drought, and that Allison Beale told her to "sell what you need to make it right." Allison Beale says no such conversation ever happened.
  Verdicts: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses: guilty
  Condition: no contact with the victim
  [Count 1: Obtaining Property by False Pretenses] Class D Felony, prior record level II (habitual felon; offense is Class H felony) | Presumptive range | Minimum 59, maximum 83 months. Active: committed to the custody of the N.C. Department of Adult Correction.
  [Commitment] Minimum term to be served before the defendant is eligible for release: 59 months (4 yr 11 mo). | Maximum term: 83 months. Earned time can move release toward the minimum, never below it; post-release supervision follows release. | Projected release: no earlier than 2 October 2031, and no later than 2 October 2032. | Upon release, the defendant will serve a period of post-release supervision (G.S. 15A-1368.2).
  [Monetary obligations] Court costs: US$213.00 | Restitution: US$26,950.00 | Appointed attorney fees: US$3,200.00 | Attorney appointment fee: US$60.00 | Total: US$30,423.00
  [Victim notification] The District Attorney's victim-witness legal assistant will notify the victim of the judgment and of any release, appeal, or post-conviction hearing (N.C. Const. art. I, § 37).
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "This is a fair result under the sentencing laws, and it's about public trust."
  Reaction: Cassandra Yamamoto: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Bryson about next steps."
  Reaction: Allison Beale's father, outside the courtroom: "Bryson will be out before we feel safe again."
  News: Bryson found guilty after closely watched bench trial — After hearing the evidence without a jury, Judge Hale found Bryson guilty, and imposed 59 to 83 months in prison. According to court records, Allison Beale leased pasture next to Yasmin Bryson's land and ran 40 head of black Angus there. Reaction outside the courthouse was split. Cassandra Yamamoto: "It's a hard day. We'll be talking with Bryson about next steps." District Attorney Mei Sloan: "This is a fair result under the sentencing laws, and it's about public trust." Allison Beale's father, outside the courtroom: "Bryson will be out before we feel safe again." Deputies led Bryson out a side door. The judgment includes US$30,423.00 in fines, fees, costs, and restitution.

=== story.bouncerFlashlight ===
TRIAL 26 CRS 006880 · State of North Carolina v. Silas Moreau
  Charges: Assault with a Deadly Weapon Inflicting Serious Injury
  Defendant: Silas Moreau, 43, Bouncer; priors: 10; Released on a $55,000 secured bond. Has made every court date.
  TEMPLATE story.bouncerFlashlight
  Summary: At closing time on 1 February, Silas Moreau, a bouncer at a bar on Water Street in Port Hollis, walked Emanuel Whaley out the back door after Emanuel Whaley refused to leave. In the alley, Silas Moreau hit Emanuel Whaley once in the head with a 14-inch steel flashlight. Emanuel Whaley fell and fractured his skull on the curb and spent nine days in the hospital. The alley camera shows Emanuel Whaley turning and raising his hands toward Silas Moreau a second before the swing, but is too grainy to show whether Emanuel Whaley was holding anything. Moreau says Emanuel Whaley had threatened to "cut" him inside and reached into his pocket. Deputies found a closed folding knife in Emanuel Whaley's jacket pocket at the hospital.
  Verdicts: Assault with a Deadly Weapon Inflicting Serious Injury: notGuilty
  Reaction: District Attorney Mei Sloan: "We presented the evidence we had. We accept the verdict."
  Reaction: Beth Broadway: "Moreau can finally go home."
  Reaction: Emanuel Whaley's sister, outside the courtroom: "We sat through every day of this. I don't know what else we could have done."
  News: Judge acquits Moreau in assault with a deadly weapon inflicting serious injury case — The State's case against Silas Moreau ended in acquittal in a case that began in Port Hollis. According to court records, at closing time on 1 February, Silas Moreau, a bouncer at a bar on Water Street in Port Hollis, walked Emanuel Whaley out the back door after Emanuel Whaley refused to leave. Two groups gathered on opposite sides of the courthouse steps. Beth Broadway: "Moreau can finally go home." District Attorney Mei Sloan: "We presented the evidence we had. We accept the verdict." Emanuel Whaley's sister, outside the courtroom: "We sat through every day of this. I don't know what else we could have done." The case was heard over 3 day.
